# Quiet Mornings

_Waiting for Lucas at the production_plan gate · project PROJECT_001_

## Quality

Package: **below A** after 3 panel rounds; 0 of 3 reviewers would approve it today. Averages: specificity 9, distinctiveness 7.7, fit 8, craft 7. (below A after 3 rounds; the open notes are listed for Lucas.) 

| Reviewer | Would approve | Spec / Dist / Fit / Craft | Verdict |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| creative_director | no | 9 / 8 / 8 / 7 | Not today, but close. The idea works. A phone flipped face-down at 7:00, a second buzz that shows up as rings in her coffee and gets nothing from her hand, and 'Everyone else gets you at 7:05.' That is dry, ownable execution. Every department serves R1, the product is a tool before it is the hero, and the taste notes are followed or broken on purpose with reasons. What stops approval is that the package doesn't agree with itself. Sound is timed to a superseded camera plan: six hits are off, and the turn's buzz trails its own picture. The treatment still carries the 18f/54f SH02A/SH02B split and wall-D card placements the camera plan can't honour. The camera plan contradicts the script's 7:05 card frame. The world bible drains away the drop the B4a beat needs. Two route departures (locked camera, neutral DTC still) wait on Lucas. I'd also cut the B2 card, so the thumb pass and the product's first look play without text and 7:05 lands as the only line. Make these fixes, get the real sample and Lucas's calls, and this goes to the client as an A. |
| film_director | no | 9 / 8 / 8 / 7 | This is a strong, unusually specific package built on an idea the brand can own. The day's second buzz reaches her coffee as rings and gets nothing back, and the 7:00 to 7:05 window turns the key message into picture and cards without a spoken word. The claims discipline is clean, with only the three verified facts on screen. I would not shoot it next week as it stands. The sound plan is synced to a superseded camera plan, with six hits off by 5-17 frames. The treatment still carries old timings and a card area the camera can't see. The SH01 grip can't physically land the phone against the mug the way QC requires. SH07's locks already fail on paper. The turn depends on rings in a 1.2 cm patch of coffee. A person also has to decide several things before anything is locked: the real product sample, the two route departures, the hand-double release and whether we build or go to a location. With the 17 notes above made and those calls in, this is an A. |
| client | no | 9 / 7 / 8 / 7 | The idea sells. Refusing the phone before the pour, the day's buzz reaching the coffee and nothing further, and the lines 'Everyone else gets you at 7:05' and 'Out from 7:00. Back at 7:05' are specific and relatable to our audience. The spot stays strictly inside our three facts and ends on a readable 3.5 s product hold with a clear CTA. I can't approve it for production today, for four reasons. The product's look, including the glaze pool the whole swap test rests on, is invented until we supply a real unit. The sound plan is synced to a camera plan that has been replaced. Three timing and placement rulings are still open. And the route departures and releases need decisions only people can make. Fix the notes above and answer the questions, and this is an 8 heading to a 9. |

## Strategy

**The Solace pour-over is the five hands-on minutes you take back for yourself before the day takes the rest of your time — a small, repeatable act of choosing slow on purpose. (This operationalizes the brief's own key message, 'A five-minute ritual that slows your morning down, on purpose,' as a proposition the creative director can build routes against.)**

- **audience tension**: The target audience — urban professionals aged 25-40 who make coffee at home and say they value ritual over convenience — live inside schedules built for speed. For most of that group, coffee on a weekday is something you get (a pod, a drip machine on autopilot, a coffee-shop stop) rather than something you make. The tension is between wanting mornings to feel intentional and self-directed, and defaulting to whatever costs the least time, because deliberately slowing down can feel indulgent or impractical on a Tuesday. The brief names this tension directly by setting 'ritual' against 'convenience' as competing values, by pricing the product at $38 (a considered purchase, not an impulse one), and by warning against generic luxury imagery — the tension is not 'I want to look rich,' it's 'I want five minutes that are mine before the day takes them.'
- **desired behavior**: A viewer sees the :15 vertical video or one of the two 4:5 stills on social, recognizes the five-minute pour-over moment as a version of a morning ritual they want for themselves (not as an ad for 'a kitchen gadget'), and takes a direct-response action: clicks through to the Solace DTC site and adds the $38 pour-over dripper to cart. The objective is explicitly DTC visits and add-to-carts, not brand-awareness alone, so the creative must carry a clear, low-friction path from feeling to action.
- **product relevance**: The ceramic pour-over cannot be a backdrop prop for a generic 'calm morning' mood — it has to be the literal mechanism that makes the ritual happen: the user stands at it, pours, waits, watches the water bloom the grounds. Its three verified attributes reinforce that role rather than sit apart from it: hand-glazed stoneware and small-batch production make the object itself read as considered and singular (supporting 'ritual', not 'appliance'), while dishwasher-safe care keeps the ritual repeatable on an ordinary Tuesday rather than precious or occasional. This is how the strategy satisfies the brief's required 'clear product role in the story' and 'readable closing product moment' without leaning on unverified performance claims (e.g., taste, extraction quality, temperature control) that were not supplied as facts.
- **proof points**: Hand-glazed stoneware ceramic (verified fact): each piece carries visible handmade variation, giving the object a tactile, one-of-a-kind quality that a mass-produced plastic or metal drip gadget cannot claim — supports 'ritual object' over 'kitchen appliance.'; Made in small batches (verified fact): reinforces a considered, non-commodity positioning consistent with a $38 price point and with the brief's instruction to avoid generic luxury imagery — the claim to make is 'made with care in limited runs,' not 'exclusive' or 'luxury.'; Dishwasher safe (verified fact): removes the plausible objection that a 'ritual' object is too precious or high-maintenance to use every weekday — the ritual survives real life, which is what makes it repeatable rather than a one-time novelty.; Structural (not brand-specific) pour-over mechanic: manual pour-over brewing requires the user's continuous attention for several minutes, unlike a pod or programmable drip machine — this is a general characteristic of the pour-over method and is treated here as a category fact, not a tested claim about the Solace product's performance, taste, or extraction quality.
- **success criteria**: Increase in click-throughs from the campaign's video and still placements to the Solace DTC product page, among the stated home-coffee-enthusiast audience segment.; Add-to-cart rate on site visits attributed to this campaign's creative, tracked against the campaign's own traffic (not an invented industry benchmark, since no baseline was supplied).; Qualitative signal that the 'ritual, on purpose' message is landing as intended — e.g., audience comments, saves, or shares that reference slowing down / morning ritual language rather than generic product-feature language.; Creative compliance checks the strategist can hand to the creative director and QC: every route keeps a clear, legible product role in the story and a readable closing product moment (both explicit brief requirements), and makes no performance, taste, or research claim beyond the three verified facts.; Note: no numeric targets (specific CTR%, add-to-cart %, conversion rate) are set here, because the brief and upstream context supplied no baseline or target figures; these should be defined by the producer/brand with actual analytics before being used as pass/fail thresholds.
- **supplied facts**: Verified product fact: hand-glazed stoneware ceramic.; Verified product fact: dishwasher safe.; Verified product fact: made in small batches.; Brief fact: price point is $38.; Brief fact: objective is to drive DTC site visits and add-to-carts among home-coffee enthusiasts.; Brief fact: audience is urban professionals aged 25-40 who make coffee at home and value ritual over convenience.; Brief fact: key message is 'a five-minute ritual that slows your morning down, on purpose.'; Brief fact: tone is quiet, warm, tactile.; Brief fact: must include a clear product role in the story and a readable closing product moment.; Brief fact: must avoid generic luxury imagery without a story, and unsupported performance claims.; Brief fact: deliverables are one 15-second 9:16 video (D01) and two 4:5 stills (D02).
- **hypotheses**: Hypothesis: this audience associates fast-format coffee (pods, programmable drip machines, coffee-shop pickup) with the rushed pace of their weekday, creating an unmet desire for a deliberately slower alternative — this is a reasoned inference from the brief's own 'ritual over convenience' framing, not sourced audience or market research.; Hypothesis: for this audience, handmade/small-batch cues signal intentionality and self-care rather than status or luxury, which is why the proposition leans on 'ritual/self-possession' rather than premium framing — inferred from the brief's tone words and its explicit instruction to avoid generic luxury imagery, not from tested creative research.; Hypothesis: removing the 'high-maintenance object' concern (via dishwasher-safe care) meaningfully lowers a real adoption barrier to daily use — a plausible behavioral read, not a validated finding.; Hypothesis: a $38 price point sits in a 'considered but attainable' zone for this audience (enough to feel intentional, not so much it reads as a luxury purchase) — an inference from the price and audience description, not a tested pricing study.

## Routes

In production: **R1**. Recommended: R1. R1 ("the line before the day") most directly dramatizes the strategy's own audience tension — ritual vs. the pull of convenience — through one specific, small, believable action (turning the phone face-down) rather than a generic mood board or an explicit before/after contrast edit. That keeps it furthest from the brief's 'generic luxury imagery without a story' risk while still giving the product an unambiguous role and a clean, legible closing shot that doubles easily as a DTC-linkable image. Its single continuous pace (no hard tonal cut) also fits the brief's 'quiet, warm, tactile' tone more precisely than R3's two-beat contrast, and it extends more naturally to both required still crops than R2's mostly-macro treatment, which risks losing legible product context if used as a static image on its own. R2 and R3 remain strong alternates: R2 is the more sensory, tactile-proof-point-forward option if the brand wants to foreground the hand-glazed/small-batch facts even more literally; R3 is the more literal, message-forward option if the producer wants the 'on purpose' contrast made explicit rather than implied.

### R1 (in production): Before the phone, the inbox, or anyone else gets a piece of you, there are five minutes that are only yours — marked by the pour.

- **emotional promise**: Quiet self-possession: the felt relief of claiming a few minutes before the day starts claiming them for you.
- **product role**: The Solace dripper is the literal boundary object. The film's one deliberate action — setting the phone face-down and reaching for the dripper instead — makes the product the mechanism of the choice, not a backdrop to it. It stays in frame through the pour, the bloom, and the final held mug, closing on an unhurried, fully lit shot of the dripper itself on the counter.
- **visual language**: Quiet and continuous, no contrast-cutting: warm low window light, muted stoneware palette (terracotta, cream, soft grey), handheld-but-steady camera, close but not macro. Sound carries more of the emotion than music — water, steam, ceramic set down, a phone buzzing once, off-screen and ignored.
- **execution example**: 15s / 9:16 (D01): Open tight on a phone screen lighting up on a counter; a hand turns it face-down without looking. Cut to the same hand lifting the Solace dripper, setting it on a mug, spooning grounds. Slow spiral pour, water blooming the grounds in close-up (2-3s held). Cut to the person at a window, mug in both hands, steam rising, a single distant notification sound goes unanswered. Final beat (2s): dripper alone on the counter in soft full light, product fully legible, with an illustrative closing line such as 'Made in small batches' (exact copy owned by copywriter). The two 4:5 stills (D02) pull directly from this beat structure: still 1 = the pour/bloom close-up with the dripper as clear hero object; still 2 = the closing counter shot, product fully visible and legible, doubling as a clean DTC-linkable product image.

### R2: The bloom itself — the moment water first meets the grounds — is the ritual's whole point, and the hand-glazed ceramic is filmed as the singular object that stages it.

- **emotional promise**: Presence: the satisfaction of doing one small thing slowly enough to actually notice it happening.
- **product role**: The dripper's surface and form are the visual subject, not just present in the shot. Macro texture on the hand-glazed stoneware (visible glaze variation, no two pieces identical) is used to make the tactile, handmade proof point legible on screen, then the frame pulls back to show the object in full use — giving the product both an intimate role and a clear closing moment.
- **visual language**: Macro and shallow depth of field, slow motion on water contact and steam, warm directional morning light, near-silent sound design (pour, drip, a single ceramic clink) with minimal or no music. No other objects compete in frame — discipline here is what keeps it from reading as generic 'luxury lifestyle' imagery.
- **execution example**: 15s / 9:16 (D01): Near-entirely macro — dry grounds catching light, first pour, the bloom rising and settling, steam curling off the surface, a fingertip resting briefly on the glaze's texture. In the last 3 seconds, camera pulls back for the first time to a clean, fully lit shot of the dripper and finished mug on the counter, with a hand entering frame to lift the mug — the human presence and the product both land together as the closing moment. Illustrative closing line: 'Hand-glazed. Small batch. Yours.' The two 4:5 stills (D02) isolate: still 1 = the bloom macro shot; still 2 = the pulled-back closing counter shot, framed as a clean product/DTC image.

### R3: An ordinary Tuesday, not a weekend — the deliberate choice to reach for the slow thing instead of the fast thing, on a day that gave no permission to slow down.

- **emotional promise**: Quiet agency: permission to choose slowness even when nothing about the day is asking for it.
- **product role**: The dripper is staged as the chosen alternative in an explicit two-beat decision (reach for the fast option, then don't — reach for this instead), which makes its role in the story unmissable. It remains the visual anchor through the pour and into a final in-focus product shot.
- **visual language**: Two-beat structure with a movement contrast rather than a lighting contrast (to avoid a glossy before/after ad feel): a slightly quicker, handheld beat for the 'fast' cue, settling into a locked-off, steady frame for the pour. Same muted warm palette throughout so the shift feels like a change in pace, not a change in world.
- **execution example**: 15s / 9:16 (D01): Opens on an out-of-focus stack of notifications/alarm on a phone, a hand starting to reach for it — then redirects to the Solace dripper sitting on the counter instead. One continuous, steady pour; the frame holds on the bloom. Final beat: person seated, mug in both hands, dripper still visible in soft foreground focus. On-screen line (illustrative, copywriter to finalize): 'A five-minute ritual, on purpose.' The two 4:5 stills (D02): still 1 = the redirected-hand moment (phone out of focus behind, dripper sharp in the foreground) carrying the tension itself; still 2 = the seated closing shot with the product legible in frame.

## Script

At 7:00 a notification lights NORA's phone. Her hand flips it face-down in passing and goes to the Solace dripper instead. When the day buzzes again, it gets as far as the rings in her coffee and no further until 7:05.

### Beat sheet

| Scene | Heading | Who | What happens | What turns |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| S1 | INT. NORA'S KITCHEN - COUNTER - 7:00 AM | NORA | Her phone lights up on the oak counter beside a chipped white mug: 7:00, and under it one notification banner too small to read. It buzzes. NORA's hand doesn't slow. It flips the phone face-down in passing, so the phone lands against the mug, and carries straight on right toward the dish rack out of frame. She lifts the Solace dripper out of the rack, and her thumb passes over the glaze pooled at its foot. We cut mid-carry, and the dripper seats on the mug with a click. | Someone wants her at 7:00 and she refuses them. The hand that refused goes to the dripper, and her five minutes start here. |
| S2 | INT. NORA'S KITCHEN - COUNTER - MOMENTS LATER | NORA | Cut to grounds already heaped in a pressed paper filter inside the dripper. NORA pours from out of frame. Only the gooseneck spout comes in from frame left, and a slow spiral lands on the grounds, which swell and dome up. Nothing else happens in frame. | The pour fills the time the flip started. The frame now belongs to the ritual, not the phone. |
| S3 | INT. NORA'S KITCHEN - COUNTER - MOMENTS LATER | NORA | A last drip falls as she lifts the dripper off the mug and sets it down just past it, so the phone, the mug and the dripper sit in a line. NORA's fingertips rest along the chipped mug's side below the rim, palm clear, with the heel of her hand on the oak counter. The phone rattles against the wood, and the coffee shivers into fine rings, with the dripper standing right there. The rattle stops, the rings flatten, and her hand stays where it is. Only then does the card come up: 'Everyone else gets you at 7:05.' | The day reaches into her cup and gets nothing. The dripper that drew the line is in frame. The card says when the day will get her: at 7:05. |
| S4 | INT. NORA'S KITCHEN - COUNTER - MOMENTS LATER |  | The mug is gone. The Solace dripper stands empty on the counter, whole and legible, and the glaze mark her thumb crossed faces the lens. The phone is still face-down and dark at the edge of frame. 'Hand-glazed stoneware.' comes on screen, then the CTA, with a small 'Solace' sign-off beneath both. | She took the coffee and left the phone. The tool becomes the hero, and the viewer is told where to buy one. |


### D01 · 15s: At 7:00 the phone goes face-down and the dripper comes up. The next buzz reaches her coffee but not her, and nobody gets her until 7:05.

| Time | Picture | Sound | Dialogue / VO | On screen |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |

The lock screen lights: 7:00 in large readable digits, with one generic notification banner beneath it, its text too small to read. It buzzes. At about 0.8s NORA's hand comes in. The hand doesn't slow: it flips the phone in passing, the phone's edge lands against the mug, and the hand carries straight on right, toward the rack. | One phone buzz rattling on the counter, then the soft knock of the case landing on wood. Then silence. |  |  |

As the hand closes on it, the thumb passes over the glaze pooled unevenly at its foot without stopping, the mark B5 later turns to the lens. The hand sets off low and level. Cut mid-carry: the dripper comes in right to left and seats with a click on the chipped white mug, which still touches the face-down phone's edge. The hand lets go and stays clear of the phone. B2 ends on the empty dripper sitting on the mug. The card sits clear of the dripper in both angles. | A faint knock as the dripper leaves the dish rack; room tone across the cut; stoneware clicking onto the mug rim. |  | Nobody gets you before the pour. |

The grounds swell, bubble and dome up against the paper. Hold on the bloom to the end of the beat. | A thin stream of water on wet grounds; the soft fizz of the bloom. No music. |  |  |

The hand sets the dripper down on the oak counter just past the mug, on the side away from the phone, so the three sit in a line: phone, mug, dripper. The card comes up at 9.5s as the rings flatten, holds to 11.5s, and sits clear of the dripper. | One phone buzz, about 1.0s long, rattling against the wood and ticking against the mug, left unanswered. The whole product is legible. Nothing in frame moves. | Room tone and the kettle ticking as it cools, to the last frame. No music, no sting. |  | Shop the Solace dripper. [sign-off] Solace |

CTA: Shop the Solace dripper. (Paid, primary: the platform's Shop Now button carries the DTC product-page link. Organic alternate card: 'Shop the Solace dripper. Link in bio.')

### Writing decisions

| Choice | Why | From |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The hook is the phone flip. The lock screen shows 7:00 and one generic notification banner too small to read, and the phone buzzes once. NORA's hand doesn't slow: it flips the phone in passing and carries straight on toward the rack, all by 1.5s. There is no VO and no card over the hook. | It is a specific action in the first frame, and it is R1's one deliberate choice. The banner makes the buzz a person wanting her rather than an alarm. With only the time on screen, a muted viewer would read the flip as a snooze. The readable 7:00 sets up the 7:05 card. | Craft brief: hook rule, 'observable action', sound-off rule; route R1 central idea: 'Before the phone, the inbox, or anyone else gets a piece of you' |
| B1 stays on the phone and the mug. The hand leaves frame right toward the dish rack, and the rack is first seen in B2's first angle. | B1 has to stay tight for 7:00 to read. The hand's exit is enough to show where the rack is. | Craft brief: sound-off rule |
| B2 is two angles with a cut mid-carry: first the lift from the rack, then the dripper seating on the mug with a click. B2 ends on that click. | Played whole, the carry can't run at habit pace in 3.0s. The cut keeps the two moments that show the product and drops the travel between them. | Brief tone: quiet; craft brief: 'Timing is a budget' |
| In B2's lift, the thumb passes over the glaze pooled at the dripper's foot and doesn't stop. B5, B6 and D02-2 turn that same mark to the lens. No card or VO points at it. | The fact is shown in use before it is named, so B5's 'Hand-glazed stoneware.' names something the viewer has already watched a hand touch. It adds the tactile note from the brief's tone, and a competitor's dripper can no longer be swapped in cleanly. Because the thumb doesn't stop, it reads as habit, not a caress for the camera. | Craft brief calibration: 'demonstrated rather than asserted'; brief tone: tactile; taste notes: 'The product is a tool, then a hero'; verified fact: hand-glazed stoneware |
| The paper filter and grounds go in during the B2/B3 cut, so B3 opens on grounds already heaped in a pressed filter. | This keeps the carry at habit pace, keeps her hand from reaching over the phone, and gives the product's first appearance time to land. | Brief key message: 'slows your morning down, on purpose'; tone: quiet |
| Every card, the sign-off and all still copy sit in negative space, clear of the dripper in every angle. | The product stays legible in every frame it appears in. | Craft brief: on-screen text never over the product |
| In B3 only the gooseneck spout comes in, from frame left. The pouring hand and the kettle body stay out of frame. D02-1 carries the pouring hand and S2's real detail, the kettle's scorch band. For B3 this breaks 'One real detail per scene' on purpose. | The bloom stays large, and it is the one held moment of the ritual. The detail isn't lost, because still 1 shows it at full size. | Taste notes: 'One real detail per scene'; route R1 execution example: bloom held 2-3s |
| New timings: B3 4.5-7.0 (the bloom holds 2.5s), B4a 7.0-8.5, B4b 8.5-11.5, B5 11.5-13.0, B6 13.0-15.0. In B4b the rattle and rings play for about 1.0s with no text. The 7:05 card comes up at 9.5s as the rings flatten and holds for 2.0s. | The turn reads from the picture first, and the card then answers it instead of announcing it. The bloom stays inside the route's 2-3s hold. The product hold is 3.5s, above the 1.5s minimum. | Craft brief: sound-off rule, 'one card per roughly 2 seconds'; taste notes: 'Restraint over explanation' |
| In B4a the dripper is set down just past the mug, so the phone, the mug and the dripper sit in a line. In B4b the dripper stands in frame on the side away from the phone while the phone rattles and the rings shiver and flatten. | The picture now shows what the beat sheet claims. The buzz reaches the cup and stops at her hand, and the tool that drew the line is right there. The dripper stays in frame through the pour, the bloom and the held mug. | Route R1 product_role; brief must-include: 'A clear product role in the story' |
| The second buzz reads through the coffee. The face-down phone rattles, and the coffee in the mug touching it shivers into fine rings. Then the rings flatten while NORA's fingertips stay put. The mug touches the phone from B1 on, because the flip lands the phone against it. | With the sound off, the turn reads from something the camera can catch in any light. Lifting the dripper off in B4a uncovers the coffee, which gives the rings a surface to show on. | Craft brief: sound-off rule; quality bar: nothing physically implausible |
| In B4b, NORA's fingertips rest along the chipped mug's side below the rim, with the palm clear and the heel of her hand on the oak counter. | A full wrap would add mass and damp the vibration, which would mute the rings the turn depends on. | Quality bar: nothing physically implausible |
| The coffee rings are the script's own real detail. The chipped mug and the dish rack stay as set texture, showing a dripper in daily use in a real kitchen. | The rings are the one thing only this story would have. | Taste notes: 'One real detail per scene'; brief must-avoid: generic luxury imagery |
| I broke from the route's execution example on purpose and cut the window-and-mug shot. B4b replaces it with the second buzz, the rings and the hand that stays put. | The window shot is the first thing the taste notes reject. The replacement keeps R1's idea and the product's role. | Taste notes: 'Anyone gazing out a window holding a mug'; route R1 product_role |
| NORA appears only as hands and forearms. No face appears in the spot or in either still. | Her hands carry the story. A face would pull the spot toward stock lifestyle footage. | Taste notes: 'Hands before faces'; craft brief: stock-lifestyle rejects |
| The spot has no VO: 0 spoken words in 15s. The 7:00-to-7:05 idea lives in the lock screen and the two cards. The words 'five minutes' are never said or written on screen. | As picture and cards, the idea reaches muted viewers, and the second buzz lands on its own in room tone. | Craft brief: timing budget and sound-off rule; taste notes: 'Restraint over explanation', 'Dry over sentimental' |
| There is no music. Real sounds carry the mood: the buzz, the case knocking on wood, the dripper leaving the rack, stoneware clicking onto the mug rim, the pour, the bloom fizz, the last drip, stoneware set down on wood, the phone ticking against the mug and the kettle ticking as it cools. | The taste notes allow music late, if at all. Without music, the second buzz stays audible, and that buzz is the turn. | Taste notes: 'Sound design before music'; route R1 visual_language |
| B1's sound is 'the soft knock of the case landing on wood'. | Face-down, the phone rests on the lip of its case, so the case touches the wood and the glass doesn't. | Quality bar: nothing physically implausible |
| The product works as a tool from B2 to B4b and is the hero in B5-B6 for 3.5s. The mug is gone in B5-B6, and the phone stays face-down and dark at the frame edge. No coffee drips show on the outer glaze. | The product is shown in use before it becomes the hero. She took the coffee and left the phone, so the last frames belong to this route. Drips on the DTC image would suggest a dripper that spills. | Brief must-include; craft brief: product moment; taste notes: 'The product is a tool, then a hero' and the ending reject |
| The B5 card reads 'Hand-glazed stoneware.' from 11.5 to 13.0s. B6 holds the CTA from 13.0 to 15.0s. | The fact is kept word for word and gets the same reading pace as the other cards. 'Made in small batches' appears on the D02-1 subline. | Brief must-include: 'A readable closing product moment'; taste notes: 'Restraint over explanation' |
| A small 'Solace' sign-off line sits beneath both end cards from 11.5 to 15.0s and stays up across the B5/B6 change. It is written after '[sign-off]' in the B5 and B6 text. It never sits over the dripper or the phone's edge. It is type on the product frame, not a logo on a coloured card. | A muted viewer can link the whole 3.5s product hold to the brand, and the product stays in frame the whole time. | Brief objective; must-include: 'A readable closing product moment'; taste notes: ending reject |
| Paid is the primary cut. The B6 card reads 'Shop the Solace dripper.', and the platform's Shop Now button carries the DTC product-page link. Both still sublines end on 'Shop the Solace dripper.' The organic alternates stay word for word: B6 'Shop the Solace dripper. Link in bio.'; D02-1 'Hand-glazed stoneware, made in small batches. Shop the Solace dripper, link in bio.'; D02-2 'Hand-glazed, and it still goes in the dishwasher. Shop the Solace dripper, link in bio.' | A specific verb with a working destination drives DTC visits and add-to-carts. | Brief objective; craft brief: CTA rule |
| The D02-2 headline is now 'Out from 7:00. Back at 7:05.' I made the still hold both ends of the window on its own rather than tying it to carousel frame 2. D02-1 keeps 'Nobody gets you before the pour.' | D02-2 is a bare plate with no phone, mug or clock, and the textless version is the DTC product image, so it will be served on its own. The line holds both clock times in 6 words. It has the dry edge of the out-of-office reply the audience writes every week, and it points straight at R1's 'the inbox'. Next to D02-1 it still reads as the film's before and after. | Brief key message; craft brief: sound-off rule (each asset reads from its own picture and text); taste notes: 'Dry over sentimental'; route R1 central idea |
| Only the three verified facts are stated. Hand-glazed stoneware is shown by the thumb in B2 and stated on the B5 card and the D02-1 subline. 'Made in small batches' is on the D02-1 subline. Dishwasher safe appears only on D02-2, as 'it still goes in the dishwasher'. Nothing is said about taste, speed or quality, and the price stays off screen. | Only verified facts may be stated. The dishwasher line turns a spec into a dry, human line. | Brief: verified product facts; must-avoid: 'Unsupported performance claims' |


## Cast

### NORA

- **role in story**: Principal and the only person on screen. She appears as hands and forearms only, seen in D01-B1, B2, B4a, B4b and D02-1. B3 shows only the spout. The script sets her name and gender. Habit: every weekday at 7:00 she makes the pour before anyone else gets any of her. Backstory, not a lock: she keeps her lists on paper rather than in the phone, which gives her a writer's callus on the right middle finger. It adds no prop, scene or copy. Playable objective: get the coffee poured and her fingertips on the mug while the phone gets nothing back from her. Age: playing 33-39, target 36. This is a choice, not a story need. It sits inside the 25-40 audience, it is old enough for the habit to look long-standing, and it reads in the creased knuckles. Heritage: not specified. The skin value is the casting choice, and a hand double is matched on the anchors, not on heritage. Defaults broken: the category's default hand is late-20s, pale and slim, with a gloss manicure and stacked gold rings on a linen cuff. The generator's stock hand is long, tapered, poreless and light-skinned. I break both because 'Hands before faces' only works if the hand isn't stock. A stock hand also drifts toward the generic luxury imagery the brief rules out.
- **screen presence**: One-frame read: a broad, square hand in its mid-thirties, with medium-deep skin, a warm undertone (#6F4A34), creased knuckles and short bare nails. Two raised veins run from the wrist across the back of each hand. They are age texture and are not locked, because heat and hand height change them. The hand flips the buzzing phone face-down in passing, without looking, and is already moving on toward the rack. The hand never searches or hovers: it lands on an object, takes it and lets go. It lifts the dripper out of the rack the way it would lift a mug it uses every day, and the thumb crosses the pooled glaze at the foot without stopping. During the pour the forearm stays still and only the wrist circles. Between actions the hand stops completely: it lies flat and doesn't tap. On the mug, the fingertips touch it without gripping it, and they stay there. Conclusion earned: this hand has done this every morning for years.
- **performance style**: Understated. Silent: no dialogue, no lip-sync, no VO. No face is cast. At rest the hand lies flat and does nothing. Every move plays at the real pace of a daily habit and is never slowed for the camera (taste note: no slow motion to make something look expensive). The flip and the carry toward the rack are one move, with no pause and no glance down. The pour is a steady, thin spiral, and the stream never wobbles or gets corrected. Her one change is the held hand at B4b. As the phone rattles against the mug, nothing in her hand moves: no flinch, no squeeze, no drift toward the phone. The rings in the coffee are the only movement under her hand, and they come from the phone, never from her grip. The mug stays on the counter. The refusal plays as stillness, not effort.
- **wardrobe fit notes**: Height 167 cm (range 165-169). She is the only principal, so there is no one to scale against. Medium build, shoulders level with the hips. The hand is about 18 cm long and 8.5 cm across the knuckles. Right-handed. The mole marks the right hand and the scar marks the left. Whichever hand the director's shot list puts on the product, that hand's mark must read. The director's restage makes the pour left-handed, so she must pour a steady, thin spiral with her left hand (tested at the casting call; see blockers). In D02-1, continuity on the pouring hand rests on the left-hand scar, the build, the skin and the knuckle creases. Product scale: in a one-hand grip on the dripper, glaze must show on both sides of the hand. So the 8.5 cm knuckle breadth must be at least 2 cm less than the dripper dimension the knuckles cross in the director's B2/B4a grip. In a grip over the rim, that is the rim diameter. In a side grip around the body, it is the height. If the dripper has a handle, the knuckles close on the handle instead, and no finger may cross the body on the lens side. If the dimension that applies is under 10.5 cm, casting reopens the hand breadth. The dimensions and the grip are pending (see blockers). Sleeves stop at least 5 cm above the wrist bone on both arms, including with an arm raised to pour, so no cuff crosses the dripper, the kettle, the stream or the mug. The sleeve must differ in value from the skin (#6F4A34) and from the glaze, so the edge of the hand reads against each. Nothing on the fingers or wrists. A ring or watch would click on stoneware over the designed sound, including the phone's tick against the mug, and a watch would add a second clock against 7:00 and 7:05. Joint lock, stylist: nails short, natural and unpolished, filed straight; right thumb cuticle slightly ragged; skin matte, with no lotion sheen; forearm hair sparse, fine and dark (#2A1E17) along the outer edge of both forearms. The stylist decides whether the hair stays or goes, and it is then the same in every setup.
- **visual identity anchors**: [macro] Build: a broad, square hand, 8.5 cm across the knuckles against a wrist about 6 cm across. Short, blunt fingers: the middle finger is about 7 cm, shorter than the palm is wide. This breaks the generator's long, tapered hand. Both hands match.; [macro] Skin: medium-deep, warm undertone, #6F4A34 on the back of the hand and the forearm, with visible pores. Palms and fingertip pads: medium, warm undertone, #B3886A. These words go with the hex codes into the hand-double call and every prompt. Reject any take that reads lighter, greyer or olive.; [micro] Knuckle creases on both hands: every knuckle is creased and darker than the skin around it (deep, warm undertone, #4A2F20). This is the locked age marker. The veins are not locked.; [micro] Right hand: a flat 4 mm mole (#3B2519) on the back of the hand, 2 cm toward the wrist from the middle-finger knuckle. The left hand has none. Reject any take with a mole on the left.; [micro] Left hand: a pale, flat, matte scar about 10 mm long (#8A6650, lighter than the skin around it) across the back of the index finger's middle joint. It shows when the hand closes on a handle. The right hand has none. Reject any take with it on the right.
- **fictional talent**: true

## World

### LOC-01

- **description**: LOC-01 · INT. NORA'S KITCHEN - COUNTER. One set carries all four script headings: S1 (D01-B1 flip; D01-B2 lift and seat), S2 (D01-B3 pour and bloom; D02-1), S3 (D01-B4a set-down; D01-B4b second buzz) and S4 (D01-B5 to D01-B6 hold; D02-2). It is a studio build. spatial_layout lists what gets built and what exists only in the story.
Place and year: USA, 2026, a pre-war rental (ASSUMED from the USD price and 'link in bio'). That means a 12-hour lock screen, a USB-C phone, a gas range and NEMA 5-15 at OUT-01, which sits below the top and in no frame. No window view, signage, street or plates appear.
History: NORA has cooked for one here for about four years (ASSUMED). The phone charged on this counter overnight, not by her bed (PROP-08). The dripper lives dry in the rack between mornings (PROP-04 ST1), and the filters live in the upper cabinet. This morning, before D01-B1, she put the kettle on RANGE-01 and set the chipped mug on its spot (PROP-03 ST1).
One real detail per scene (taste notes):
- S1: the tide line inside the empty mug, 2 cm below the rim, the level she fills to every day (PROP-03 ST1).
- S2: the kettle's gas-scorch band. It is seen in D02-1 only, because the script drops it from D01-B3 on purpose.
- S3: the coffee rings bending TILE-01's reflected edge, with the phone against the mug (the script's own detail).
- S4: the phone left face-down where she flipped it, its mug-side edge now meeting bare oak where the mug stood. Mark 2 is texture only. D02-2 stays bare on purpose as the DTC image.
The rack, mug and cable are texture ('Real kitchens have a dish rack and a charging cable'). The window is never in frame, and nobody looks out of it.
Blank surfaces (generators add logos here): dripper exterior, phone case and back, phone glass except the lock screen, kettle, mug, plate, tumbler, rack and tray, filters, cable, adapter, grommet ring, cabinet fronts, and TILE-01 (no pattern, decal or maker mark).
Only text: the D01-B1 lock screen, composited, top toward 90°. It shows '7:00' in #1B1F24 generic sans on a flat #E4E7EA field (13:1). Below that is one generic banner: a rounded #FFFFFF bar holding a blank #B8BDC3 icon square and two #8A9097 lines too small to read, with no glyphs. There is no date or status bar.
Motion: hands, water, steam (D01-B3 to D01-B4b), coffee rings (D01-B4b) and phone light (D01-B1, D01-B4b). Nothing moves in D01-B5 to D01-B6 or in D02-2.
Fallback: the dressing is flop-safe (no legible set text, a centred lens cut-out), and the lock screen is composited after any flop.
Frame locks list the props besides the product, 5 at most. PROP-07 is the surface in every frame and is not counted.
- D01-B1: 02/03/08.
- D01-B2: 02/03/04/08.
- D01-B3: 02 (its face-down wall-D end)/03/05/06. PROP-08 is plugged in, off frame.
- D01-B4a: 02/03/05 (ST5, spent filter in the cone)/08.
- D01-B4b: 02/03/05 (ST5)/08.
- D01-B5 to D01-B6: 02 only. PROP-08 is plugged in, off frame.
- D02-1: 02/03/05/06, with PROP-08 struck.
- D02-2: none.
No canister, spoon, filter stack or filter box is in any frame. The filter and grounds go in during the D01-B2/D01-B3 cut, as the script decides.
Palette shares are of the D01-B4a counter view. The 8% outside the palette is NORA's hand and sleeve (cast and wardrobe) and coffee #3B2416. Per-frame note for D01-B3 and D02-1: PROP-06 #A4583F terracotta matte enamel is the accent at about 12% of frame (0% of the master). The set breaks no taste note; the D01-B3 break is the script's.
- **spatial layout**: Studio BUILD for a small crew (ASSUMED).
Story room: 2.6 W × 3.4 D × 2.75 H m. Walls run clockwise in plan: A entrance (y 0), B (x 0), C window (y 340), D counter (x 260). The origin is corner A/B; x runs along A toward D and y toward C, in cm, and heights are from the floor. Bearings: 0° = toward wall C, clockwise, so 90° faces the wall-D backsplash and 270° faces wall B. With the run on wall D, the rack lies at frame right of the mug as seen from the aisle, so the D01-B2 carry runs right to left as D01-B1 and D01-B2 say.
Openings:
- DOOR-01, wall A, x 110-192: one 82 cm leaf hinged at x 192, swinging out into the hall. The view through it is the unlit hall, which is in no frame.
- WIN-01, wall C, x 160-250, sill 100 cm, head 215 cm. No sash, glass or translight. A diffusion frame fills the opening as the key's position (the unit and the diffusion are the DP's), so nothing beyond it can be seen.
Plan (story):
- Wall D, starting from wall A: a base cabinet at y 0-40. Then RANGE-01, a 76 cm four-burner gas range at x 196-260, y 40-116, with a hood above. The kettle heats on its front burner at 212/97, which causes the scorch band, and ticks there off frame in D01-B5 to D01-B6, about 1.8 m toward wall A from the dripper. Then the counter run at y 116-340, with the sink at y 125-185 and FAUCET-01, a single-lever gooseneck at 254/155 (the knock that chipped the mug).
- FRIDGE-01, 61 × 70 cm, in corner B/C at x 0-61, y 270-340, front facing 180°.
- The aisle, x 70-196, stays clear to 2.1 m.
BUILT:
(1) PROP-07 counter run on wall D: y 116-340, x 197-260, top at 91 cm, on plain base cabinets with blank slab fronts. The sink cut-out is at x 204-250, y 125-185, dry and empty (no dish, sponge, soap or brush), with Mark 3 at its front corner.
(2) Wall-D backsplash flat: y 116-340, floor to 275 cm. Upper cabinets at y 116-200 only, x 227-260, 145-230 cm, with a soffit to the ceiling. Nothing hangs over the work zone.
(3) TILE-01 on wall D: one course of 7.5 × 15 cm matte #D5D8D2 tile laid horizontally at y 250-340, bottom edge at 112.0 cm, top edge at 119.5 cm, with #C4C6C0 grout and a bullnose end at y 250. It is left from an older splash whose lower courses were skimmed and painted (ASSUMED). It stays empty: nothing leans on it or hangs from it, so it adds no lock.
(4) Bare #6B6E67 flats, floor to 275 cm, for the gloss mirror paths: wall B (x 0, y 0-340); wall A (y 0, x 0-260, solid, because DOOR-01 is story only); and wall C (y 340, x 0-260, with the WIN-01 opening). Walls A and B are wild for access and close for the hero setups.
(5) CEIL-01: a flat matte #E6E2DA ceiling at 275 cm over x 0-260, y 0-340. The DP may cut lamp openings; the prelight confirms that no gloss surface sees one.
NOT BUILT (story only, in no frame): RANGE-01 and its hood, FRIDGE-01, FAUCET-01, DOOR-01 and the base cabinet at y 0-40. Any frame that shows y < 185 needs FAUCET-01 built. The kettle is heated off-set. The WIN-01 translight is struck.
OUT-01: a NEMA 5-15 duplex with an ivory plate, on the wall-D flat inside the base cabinet at y 322, 30 cm above the floor. It is below the top and in no frame. PROP-08 reaches it through a Ø 2.0 cm hole in the top's back strip, centred at 258/322 and lined with a flush black grommet #2A2C2E. Nothing runs on wall D above the top.
Card area: the wall-D paint from 125 cm up, at y 240-340, stays bare for composited cards. TILE-01 stops 5.5 cm below it.
Coffee mirror line (D01-B4b): the coffee surface is at 98.5 cm at 232.7/295, and wall D is 27.3 cm behind it along x. A lens at height h and horizontal distance d from the mug sees wall D in the coffee at 98.5 + (h − 98.5) × 27.3/d cm. TILE-01's 112.0 cm edge lands mid-coffee at h = 98.5 + 0.494·d: 118.3 cm at d 40, 123.2 at d 50, 128.2 at d 60. The 119.5 cm edge needs h = 98.5 + 0.769·d. Off the x axis, replace 27.3 with 27.3/sin(bearing of the lens-to-mug line). The mirror point on wall D then sits at y = 295 + 27.3/tan(bearing), which is inside TILE-01's y 250-340 for bearings of 31-149°. Lens placement is the DP's.
TILE-01 and the dripper: the rim tops out at 99.5 cm. For any lens at or above 99.5 cm, the dripper's outline is backed by oak or by paint below 99.5 cm and never by TILE-01, a margin of 12.5 cm. A lower hero angle needs a re-check.
Marks (centre x/y). Every mark is hard, and this is the one set for every department.
- PROP-07: 15 oak strips of 4.2 cm running along y, with seams at x = 260 − 4.2n.
- PROP-04 rack: x 220-252, y 190-236. PROP-01 stands in it at 232.7/213.
- PROP-03 mug and PROP-01 ST2-ST3: 232.7/295, 45 cm from wall C.
- PROP-01 ST4-ST5: 232.7/280, 60 cm from wall C, on clean oak. The D01-B4a set-down lands within ±1 cm, and props reset it exactly for D01-B5.
- Carries run along the x 232.7 strip centre: 82 cm in D01-B2 and 15 cm in D01-B4a.
- PROP-02 phone: long axis along x, top toward 90°, port at the aisle end. ST1 is face-up at 230.0/304.8, port at 222.3/304.8. ST2-ST4 is face-down at 230.0/302.8 (case x 222.3-237.7, y 299.0-306.6), its mug-side edge touching the mug at 232.7/299, port at 222.3/302.8.
- PROP-08 route: from the port, out along the phone's axis to x 218. Then along x 218 toward wall C through its loop (x 210-218, y 308-318 in ST1; x 211-218, y 308-316 in ST2) to y 322. Then along y 322 to x 258, and down the grommet to OUT-01. It never enters y < 302 and never runs up wall D.
- PROP-07 Mark 2 (texture): front edge, x 197-201, y 250-330.
The foot (x 227.7-237.7) straddles the seams at 230.6 and 234.8, each 2.1 cm off centre. Clear zone (D01-B5 to D01-B6, D02-2): x 221.7-243.7, y 269-291, 0-25 cm above the top. The phone lies 8 cm beyond it.
- **palette**: #533C2B white-oak butcher block finished with a tobacco-brown tinted hardwax oil, matte (PROP-07 top; the builder matches a sample board to this hex under the 18% card), with #3B2B1F hairline glue seams. Dominant, 45% of the master (D01-B4a counter view). Untinted oiled white oak ambers to about #B08A5E and would drop the product body to 2.3:1. The earlier #7A5A3E left the glaze pool at 2.3:1 and the raw flange at 1.9:1.; #6B6E67 grey-green landlord paint: washable eggshell on every flat and the backsplash, semi-gloss on the upper cabinets and base fronts. Secondary, 27%.; #F2EFE8 white gloss glaze (PROP-03 mug, PROP-04 plate), with #F1EEE6 bleached crepe paper (PROP-05 collar) and #D5D8D2 pale sage-grey matte tile (TILE-01, the one course on wall D). Neutral, 9%. The #E6E2DA flat ceiling (CEIL-01) is mirror path only, 0% of any frame.; #E3D9C6 oatmeal-cream satin glaze with #5A4636 iron speck, pooling to #B59A72 gloss, with a #C9A77E gloss rim band and a raw #A88663 matte foot. PROP-01 only. Accent, 7%. No other object on the set may carry this cream; TILE-01 is a cool sage-grey.; #77756F stone-grey matte silicone, the PROP-02 bumper (2.2:1 on the oak). Neutral, 3%.; #2A2C2E black matte TPE, the PROP-08 cable jacket and the grommet ring (1.4:1 on the oak). Neutral, 1%.
- **product placement**: Where it works: in the PROP-07 work zone at the wall-C end of the counter, never on a plinth.
- It enters standing in the rack at 232.7/213 and is first seen in D01-B2's first angle; the D01-B1 hand exits toward it.
- It brews on PROP-03 at 232.7/295, 45 cm from wall C (D01-B2 seat to D01-B4a; D02-1).
- It rests at 232.7/280, 60 cm from wall C, on the far side of the mug from the phone, so the phone, the mug and the dripper sit in a line (D01-B4a to D01-B6; D02-2).
D01-B2 carries it 82 cm and D01-B4a carries it 15 cm, both along the x 232.7 strip centre and never turned in the hand. The front (the thickest 4 cm arc of the glaze pool, the mark her thumb crosses in D01-B2) faces 235°, toward the aisle and wall A, in every state.
Its job changes PROP-04 ST1→ST2 and PROP-03 ST1→ST2 in D01-B2. It takes the filter in the D01-B2/D01-B3 cut (PROP-05 ST2), and its D01-B4a set-down changes PROP-07 ST1→ST2.

Swap test: the uneven glaze pool at the foot (gloss #B59A72, 3-12 mm). It is under her thumb in D01-B2 and faces the lens at 235° in D01-B5, D01-B6 and D02-2. It exists because the piece is hand-glazed (verified); an evenly machine-glazed same-tier cone has a level glaze line. It is the only swap test.

Contrast under an even key (18% grey card #767676):
- Body #E3D9C6 on the oak #533C2B: 7.3:1. On the backsplash #6B6E67: 3.7:1.
- At the base, on the oak: the pool is 3.8:1 and the raw flange edge #A88663 is 3.1:1. The pool on the flange is 1.25:1, carried by gloss over matte.
- On the white mug #F2EFE8 the body is only 1.2:1. The raw band hanging 2 mm outside the rim separates them at 2.9:1, and the pool at 2.3:1: a finish change from satin to gloss to raw.
- Pool against the body: 1.9:1, plus satin against gloss.
- Top edge in D01-B3 and D02-1: the collar #F1EEE6 on the rim band #C9A77E is 1.9:1, plus crepe against gloss. The collar on the wall is 4.5:1, and the bloom in the white filter is 13.8:1.
- Everything else in the hero frame reads lower than the body: the phone #77756F on the oak at 2.2:1, the cable #2A2C2E at 1.4:1 (off frame in D01-B5 to D01-B6), and TILE-01 on the paint at 3.6:1, never behind the dripper's outline.

Mirror path, in section and plan. Only the pool, the rim band and the mug glaze are gloss (about 5%). The pool fills the fillet between the cone wall (normal 18° below horizontal) and the flange top (normal straight up), so in section it faces every elevation from −18° to 90°. For a lens at elevation θ above the pool (92 cm) on the front's 235° bearing, a normal at elevation φ mirrors elevation 2φ − θ; values above 90° carry over the zenith onto the far side (bearing 55°).
- Below 0°: the oak top in front of the foot, out to the front edge (x 197, 44 cm).
- 0° to 32.8°: the wall-B flat at y ≈ 117, 92-275 cm (the flat runs full height, so there is no gap).
- At θ: the lens itself; a flag with a lens port is the DP's.
- 32.8° to 90°: CEIL-01, from wall B to overhead.
- 90° to 100°: CEIL-01 over the counter to wall D.
- 100° to 180° − θ: wall D from 275 cm down to 92 + 33.3·tan θ cm, which is 104 cm at θ 20°, 111 at 30° and 120 at 40°. From θ ≈ 30°, the pool's up-facing top catches TILE-01's 112 cm edge as a thin pale line on the gloss, which helps the swap test read.
In plan, the front's ±30° (normals 205-265°) fans the reflection over bearings 175-295°: down the counter to the wall-A flat (175-220°), the wall-B flat (220-285°) and the wall-C flat beside WIN-01 (285-295°). The flank toward wall D (normals 145-205°) sees the backsplash, TILE-01, the upper-cabinet end panel and the rack. The flank toward wall C (normals about 272-306°) sees the WIN-01 opening, which is the key's diffusion frame and so the DP's glint. At the pool's size (3-12 mm) only tone masses read, so bare flats stand in for the unbuilt range, fridge and door. The coffee mirrors wall D: bare paint and TILE-01's edge, with no outlet or cable. Any light in these paths is a DP request.

Clear zone for D01-B5 to D01-B6 and D02-2: x 221.7-243.7, y 269-291, 0-25 cm above the top. Nothing in it moves. The phone lies 8 cm beyond it and reads lower than the product, and the cable is off frame. No plaques, tags, price cards or packaging appear, and the exterior carries no logo. Supers are composited and never placed over the dripper. No paid image shows a mark the product made.

| PROP-01 | Solace pour-over dripper, the product. Shape and size are ASSUMED from ceramic size-02 cones; no dimensions, colour or finish were supplied. Form: a handle-less cone, 8.5 cm tall, rim Ø 11.5 cm, 7.5 cm deep to a single 6 mm exit hole 5 mm above the underside. The outer wall is about 18° off vertical, Ø 6.5 cm where it meets the flange. Base flange: outer Ø 10.0 cm, central opening Ø 4.5 cm, outer edge 4 mm tall, top at 0.6 cm. Material: hand-glazed stoneware (verified). Glaze: the body is oatmeal-cream satin #E3D9C6 with #5A4636 iron speck (0.5-1.5 mm, about 15 per 10 cm²). Over the rim it breaks thin to a 2 mm gloss band #C9A77E. At the foot it pools unevenly to gloss #B59A72, 3-12 mm tall, filling the fillet between the cone wall and the flange top. The thickest 4 cm arc of that pool is the 'front': the mark her thumb crosses in D01-B2 and the only swap-test detail. Raw foot (ASSUMED): the flange edge and a 0.8 cm underside band (Ø 10.0 outer, 8.4 inner) are unglazed #A88663 matte. The band stands 1.5 mm proud, so only the band touches a flat counter. On the Ø 8.0 mug the band hangs 2 mm outside the rim and centres the dripper, glazed underside on glazed rim (the D01-B2 click). Markings: no exterior logo or text. Any maker's stamp goes on the underside, which no frame sees. Appears in no other location. | The front faces 235° in every state; it is never turned in the hand. ST1 (D01-B2 first angle, until the lift; the D01-B1 hand exits toward it): upright in PROP-04 at 232.7/213, dry. ST2 (D01-B2, not before D01-B2): lifted, with the thumb passing over the front glaze pool without stopping. It is carried 82 cm along x 232.7 and seated empty on PROP-03 at 232.7/295, the raw band hanging 2 mm outside the rim. D01-B2 ends on it empty (script). ST3 (D01-B3; D02-1): same spot. The filter and grounds went in during the D01-B2/D01-B3 cut (PROP-05 ST2 to ST4). ST4 (D01-B4a to D01-B4b, not before D01-B3): lifted, and the last drip falls from its tip into the coffee. Props drain the cone before the take, so nothing else drips. It is carried 15 cm along x 232.7 and set down at 232.7/280 within ±1 cm, on clean oak, with the spent filter (PROP-05 ST5) still in the cone. D01-B4b holds wherever the take lands. ST5 (D01-B5 to D01-B6, not before D01-B4b): props reset it exactly on 232.7/280 across the script's 'moments later' cut, with the foot straddling the seams at 230.6 and 234.8 symmetrically. The filter is out and it is empty. The outer glaze is dry, with no coffee, fingerprints or crumbs. D02-2: ST5, with the mug, phone and cable struck. |
| PROP-02 | NORA's phone: a generic 2026 slab, 15 × 7.2 × 0.9 cm. Case: a stone-grey matte silicone bumper #77756F (15.4 × 7.6 × 1.1 cm) with a blank back plate and one centred rectangular lens cut-out at the top showing two black lenses. It is mirror-symmetric and flop-safe, with no logo and no raised multi-lens module. Face side: only four corner pads stand about 1.5 mm proud of the glass, and the bumper is open along all four edges between them. Face-down, the case touches the wood and the glass doesn't: the glass rides 1.5 mm off the oak, which gives the D01-B4b glow a path. USB-C port at the bottom. Its only screen state is the lock screen given in the LOC-01 text spec. The case reads 2.2:1 on the oak, so the S4 phone reads while staying below the product body's 7.3:1. Charcoal would read 1.0:1 on the deepened oak and vanish. ST4 (D01-B5 to D01-B6, not before D01-B4b): face-down and dark at 230.0/302.8, unmoved since the flip. Its mug-side edge now meets bare oak where the mug stood (the S4 detail), 8 cm outside the clear zone. D02-2: struck. |
| PROP-03 | NORA's chipped white mug: a straight-sided diner mug, Ø 8.0 × 9.5 cm, about 330 ml, white gloss #F2EFE8 over a buff body, with no print. Chip: on the rim at bearing 195°, 7 × 4 mm and 2 mm deep, raw buff #CDBBA0 matte against the gloss, from a knock on FAUCET-01 while rinsing. Tide line: inside, 2 cm below the rim, #B79C7A.
